What's happening | Metric | Value | Problem | |--------|-------|---------| | Power source detected | USB (not AC) | Should be AC wall charger | | Charging current | 500mA (2.5W) | Fire HD 10 ships with 9W charger | | Current draw | ~700mA | Device uses MORE than it receives | | Battery level right now | 27% | Draining while "plugged in" | | Battery cycles | 97 | Moderate wear | Your tablet is **slowly dying even while plugged in** because it's only receiving 500mA but consuming ~700mA with the screen on and Fully Kiosk running. The charger is being detected as a basic USB source instead of a wall charger. When the battery eventually hits 0%, it powers off completely. The battery stats confirm this pattern - the charge level bounces around but trends downward over days until shutdown. ## Why it started 4 months ago After years of 24/7 connection, the **cable, charger, or USB port** has degraded enough that proper power negotiation fails. The charger's data pins (D+/D-) are how the tablet identifies it as a wall charger vs basic USB. When those degrade, it falls back to 500mA USB spec. ## Fixes (in order of priority) **1. Replace the cable first** (cheapest, most likely culprit). Get a quality USB-C cable that supports at least 2A. **2. Replace the charger** if a new cable doesn't fix it. Any 5V/2A+ adapter will work, but the Amazon 9W original equivalent is ideal. **3. Clean the USB-C port** on the tablet with compressed air - lint/dust buildup can cause poor contact. Want me to also run some ADB commands to reduce power consumption in the meantime (lower brightness, disable unnecessary Amazon bloatware, etc.) so the 500mA can at least keep up until you get a new cable? (1)[409:fuelgauged]get_shutdown_cond ret: 0 0 0 0 vbat:3916 Here's the full picture now: ## Diagnosis: Amazon's software is shutting down your tablet The evidence is clear: - **Shutdown reason**: `Software_Shutdown` (confirmed in kernel logs, dropbox, and Vitals) - **Boot reason**: `reboot,userrequested` (system software initiated) - **Last session**: ran for ~20.4 hours before shutdown - **The OTA service** (`com.amazon.device.software.ota`) is actively running on every boot - **A forced OTA package** (`com.amazon.kindle.otter.oobe.forced.ota`) exists as a privileged system app listening for `START_FORCED_OTA` intents - Your `ota_disable_automatic_update=1` setting is being overridden or bypassed Amazon is almost certainly triggering periodic shutdowns as part of a failed OTA update cycle. It tries to install an update, shuts the device down to apply it, and since updates are blocked/unavailable, the device just stays off. ## Fix: Disable the OTA packages via ADB Want me to run these commands to disable the offending packages? ``` adb shell pm disable-user --user 0 com.amazon.device.software.ota adb shell pm disable-user --user 0 com.amazon.device.software.ota.override adb shell pm disable-user --user 0 com.amazon.kindle.otter.oobe.forced.ota adb shell pm disable-user --user 0 com.amazon.settings.systemupdates ``` This will prevent the OTA services from running without needing root, and is reversible if you ever want to re-enable them. It won't affect anything else on the device since you're using it as a dedicated kiosk. yes Exit code 255 Security exception: Cannot disable a protected package: com.amazon.device.software.ota java.lang.SecurityException: Cannot disable a protected package: com.amazon.device.software.ota at com.android.server.pm.PackageManagerService.setEnabledSetting(PackageManagerService.java:21114) at com.android.server.pm.PackageManagerService.setApplicationEnabledSetting(PackageManagerService.java:21025) at com.android.server.pm.PackageManagerShellCommand.runSetEnabledSetting(PackageManagerShellCommand.java:1617) at com.android.server.pm.PackageManagerShellCommand.onCommand(PackageManagerShellCommand.java:208) at android.os.ShellCommand.exec(ShellCommand.java:103) at com.android.server.pm.PackageManagerService.onShellCommand(PackageManagerService.java:21668) at android.os.Binder.shellCommand(Binder.java:634) at android.os.Binder.onTransact(Binder.java:532) at android.content.pm.IPackageManager$Stub.onTransact(IPackageManager.java:2796) at com.android.server.pm.PackageManagerService.onTransact(PackageManagerService.java:3975) at android.os.Binder.execTransact(Binder.java:731) <tool_use_error>Cancelled: parallel tool call Bash(adb shell pm disable-user --user 0 com.a

name="android.permission.INTERACT_ACROSS_USERS"/> </privapp-permissions> <privapp-permissions package="com.amazon.device.sync"> <permission name="android.permission.DUMP"/> There it is. The OTA service has **`android.permission.REBOOT`** and **`android.permission.RECOVERY`** as privileged permissions. These cannot be revoked without root. Amazon's OTA service can shut down your device at will, and there's no ADB-level way to stop it on a non-rooted Fire tablet. ## Here's the situation and your real options **Why it can't be fixed without root:** The OTA packages are "protected" at the framework level. Can't disable them, can't uninstall them, can't revoke their reboot permission. Amazon designed it this way. ### Option 1: Block OTA at the network level (easiest, no root needed) Since you already run Home Assistant, you likely have access to your router or run something like AdGuard/Pi-hole. Block these domains: - `softwareupdates.amazon.com` - `device-software-updates-us.amazon.com` - `firs-ta-g7g.amazon.com` - `amzn-ota-eu.amazon.com` - `amzn-ota-na.amazon.com` If the OTA service can't reach Amazon's servers, it shouldn't trigger a shutdown to apply a non-existent update. ### Option 2: Root the device (permanent fix) Your tablet has a MediaTek MT8183 SoC (confirmed from kernel logs). The Fire HD 10 11th gen ("trona") has known root methods via `mtk-su` or the `amonet` bootloader exploit. Once rooted, we can fully remove the OTA packages from the system partition. ### Option 3: Smart plug auto-recovery (workaround) Use a smart plug controlled by Home Assistant. Monitor the tablet (via ping or Fully Kiosk's REST API), and if it goes offline, power-cycle the plug. Fire tablets auto-boot when power is reconnected. | XDA Forums","url":"https://xdaforums.com/t/fire-hd-10-11th-gen-root.4368441/"}] Based on the search results, here's what I found regarding the Fire HD 10 11th gen (Trona) and rooting methods: ## Current Status The latest unlocking methods (amonet, kamakiri) exploited bootrom to achieve arbitrary access to the eMMC, however, Amazon has disabled bootrom on their newer devices through OTA updates. This significantly impacts rooting possibilities for the Fire HD 10 11th generation. ## Technical Challenges This particular tablet has BROM (bootrom) access disabled, which eliminates the use of tools like MTKClient, even though the device has a Helio P60 (MT8183) which is supported by MTKClient. Additionally, the Trona tablet has a fastboot option, but fastboot commands are disabled. ## Alternative Approaches Potential research directions include finding an exploit in the preloader (which is still accessible) or a temporary root shell, though this would require finding an exploit that fits the kernel. ## Older Generations For context, on older Fire HD models, you could extract files and run MTK-SU.bat to install SuperSU app and root the device, then set default root access in settings, but this approach does not appear viable for the 11th generation. VPN Firewall Approach:** You can use NoRoot Firewall (VPN firewall) from Google Play, enable Stat on Boot, create VPN connection, select APPS tab, find DeviceSoftwareOTA app from list and set the check box to X (deny any internet access) on Wi-Fi and mobile data. **2. ADB Command Method:** If you enable developer options and USB debugging, then open an ADB shell on an attached PC and run the command `pm disable-user --user 0 com.amazon.device.software.ota` this disables OTA firmware updates. **3. OTA Blocker Directory:** To prevent OTA updates, you can create the OTA blocker directory (folder) name to the top of visible USB storage. Create an empty folder in the root folder, and name as "update.bin.tmp" (if firmware version older than 5.6.x), or "update.bin.tmp.partial" (since firmware version 5.6.x until 5.8.x). ## Root-Based Methods: For rooted devices, you can run the command `pm disable-user --user 0 com.amazon.device.software.ota` and `pm disable-user --user 0 com.amazon.kindle.otter.oobe.forced.ota` to disable both OTA services. Note that Amazon tries to prevent this type of update blocking, so whether this method will work for all the firmwares depends on your own tests. NetGuard is an open-source firewall app that allows you to block WiFi and mobile data access for selected apps, and has been used to stop updates on FireStick and Fire tablets. **How It Works:** NetGuard uses a local VPN to block connections, which means it can function without root privileges. You can use NetGuard to block the necessary Amazon OTA update services whilst on WiFi. **Specific Packages to Block:** When using NetGuard, you can enable blocking system apps and search for OTA-related packages, then block: DeviceSoftwareOTA, Forced OTA, and System Updates. **Important Limitations:** However, there are some practical concerns: Despite claims to the contrary no local 'firewall' can be 100% effective without root access. Additionally, one user reported that Netguard app to block all Amazon-related activity still allowed one update to download, indicating it might not be entirely reliable. **VPN Conflict:** NetGuard cannot run alongside any VPN service - if you use a VPN on your Fire tablet, NetGuard will be automatically turned off. Your tablet's IP is `[REDACTED-LAN-IP]` (from the ADB output earlier). You can add filtering rules that only apply to that IP. In your AdGuard Home, go to **Filters > Custom filtering rules** and add these: ``` ||softwareupdates.amazon.com^$client=[REDACTED-LAN-IP] ||updates.amazon.com^$client=[REDACTED-LAN-IP] ||d1s31zyz7dcc2d.cloudfront.net^$client=[REDACTED-LAN-IP] ||d1s31zyz7dcc2d.cloudfront.prod.ota-cloudfront.net^$client=[REDACTED-LAN-IP] ||d2jmp5j6kma5v6.cloudfront.net^$client=[REDACTED-LAN-IP] ``` Only the tablet gets blocked. Your other Fire devices are unaffected. One thing to consider: make sure the tablet has a **static IP** (either set on the tablet or as a DHCP reservation on your router), otherwise if the IP changes, these rules stop applying. Found the cause. The Fire HD 10's hardware H.264 decoder is being overwhelmed. Look at these errors repeating: ``` Too many frames backed up in the decoder, dropping frame Failed to decode frame, error code: -1 No decodable frame in 200 ms, requesting keyframe ``` The cameras are using **WebRTC streams**, and the MT8183 SoC in your Fire HD 10 can only handle ~1-2 simultaneous 1080p H.264 decode streams. Your dashboard is showing 5+ cameras at once, which saturates the decoder. When frames fail to decode, WebRTC renders the default fallback color: **green**. Your phone handles it fine because it has a beefier decoder. ## Fixes, in order of effectiveness **1. Switch those camera cards to `picture-entity` (easiest)** Change the Nest/Wyze cards from streaming/WebRTC cards to `picture-entity` with a snapshot refresh (e.g., every 5-10s). No decoder load, no green screens. You'll lose live video but get auto-refreshing stills. **2. Reduce simultaneous streams** Only put 1-2 WebRTC streams on the main dashboard. Move the rest to sub-dashboards where you click through one at a time. **3. Try disabling hardware acceleration in Fully Kiosk** In Fully Kiosk: **Settings 
 Web Content Settings 
 Hardware Acceleration 
 OFF**. The software decoder is slower but more reliable, and without HW decoding the green-fallback issue usually disappears. **4. Lower the stream resolution for the tablet** If you're using go2rtc, add a lower-res stream profile for the kiosk view (e.g., 480p). Less work for the decoder. Here's the diagnosis. **The root cause is a charging problem, not Amazon killing your device.** ## What's happening | Metric | Value | Problem | |--------|-------|---------| | Power source detected | USB (not AC) | Should be AC wall charger | | Charging current | 500mA (2.5W) | Fire HD 10 ships with 9W charger | | Current draw | ~700mA | Device uses MORE than it receives | | Battery level right now | 27% | Draining while "plugged in" | | Battery cycles | 97 | Moderate wear | Your tablet is **slowly dying even while plugged in** because it's only receiving 500mA but consuming ~700mA with the screen on and Fully Kiosk running. The charger is being detected as a basic USB source instead of a wall charger. When the battery eventually hits 0%, it powers off completely. The battery stats confirm this pattern - the charge level bounces around but trends downward over days until shutdown. ## Why it started 4 months ago After years of 24/7 connection, the **cable, charger, or USB port** has degraded enough that proper power negotiation fails. The charger's data pins (D+/D-) are how the tablet identifies it as a wall charger vs basic USB. When those degrade, it falls back to 500mA USB spec. ## Fixes (in order of priority) **1. Replace the cable first** (cheapest, most likely culprit). Get a quality USB-C cable that supports at least 2A. **2. Replace the charger** if a new cable doesn't fix it. Any 5V/2A+ adapter will work, but the Amazon 9W original equivalent is ideal. **3. Clean the USB-C port** on the tablet with compressed air - lint/dust buildup can cause poor contact. Here's the full picture now: ## Diagnosis: Amazon's software is shutting down your tablet The evidence is clear: - **Shutdown reason**: `Software_Shutdown` (confirmed in kernel logs, dropbox, and Vitals) - **Boot reason**: `reboot,userrequested` (system software initiated) - **Last session**: ran for ~20.4 hours before shutdown - **The OTA service** (`com.amazon.device.software.ota`) is actively running on every boot - **A forced OTA package** (`com.amazon.kindle.otter.oobe.forced.ota`) exists as a privileged system app listening for `START_FORCED_OTA` intents - Your `ota_disable_automatic_update=1` setting is being overridden or bypassed Amazon is almost certainly triggering periodic shutdowns as part of a failed OTA update cycle. It tries to install an update, shuts the device down to apply it, and since updates are blocked/unavailable, the device just stays off. ## Fix: Disable the OTA packages via ADB Want me to run these commands to disable the offending packages? This will prevent the OTA services from running without needing root, and is reversible if you ever want to re-enable them. It won't affect anything else on the device since you're using it as a dedicated kiosk.
yesF
There it is. The OTA service has **`android.permission.REBOOT`** and **`android.permission.RECOVERY`** as privileged permissions. These cannot be revoked without root. Amazon's OTA service can shut down your device at will, and there's no ADB-level way to stop it on a non-rooted Fire tablet. ## Here's the situation and your real options **Why it can't be fixed without root:** The OTA packages are "protected" at the framework level. Can't disable them, can't uninstall them, can't revoke their reboot permission. Amazon designed it this way. ### Option 1: Block OTA at the network level (easiest, no root needed) Since you already run Home Assistant, you likely have access to your router or run something like AdGuard/Pi-hole. Block these domains: - `softwareupdates.amazon.com` - `device-software-updates-us.amazon.com` - `firs-ta-g7g.amazon.com` - `amzn-ota-eu.amazon.com` - `amzn-ota-na.amazon.com` If the OTA service can't reach Amazon's servers, it shouldn't trigger a shutdown to apply a non-existent update. ### Option 2: Root the device (permanent fix) Your tablet has a MediaTek MT8183 SoC (confirmed from kernel logs). The Fire HD 10 11th gen ("trona") has known root methods via `mtk-su` or the `amonet` bootloader exploit. Once rooted, we can fully remove the OTA packages from the system partition. ### Option 3: Smart plug auto-recovery (workaround) Use a smart plug controlled by Home Assistant. Monitor the tablet (via ping or Fully Kiosk's REST API), and if it goes offline, power-cycle the plug. Fire tablets auto-boot when power is reconnected. Which approach do you want to go with?
Good - clearing the data succeeded for all three services. That removes any pending update state that may have been queued up to trigger the next shutdown. Here's what I've done so far on the tablet: 1. **Cleared data** for OTA service, forced OTA, and DCPMS (removes pending update state) 2. **Created OTA blocker directory** (`/sdcard/update.bin.tmp.partial`) to prevent OTA downloads 3. **Force-stopped** all OTA services 4. **Confirmed OTA disable setting** is active But these services will restart on boot and eventually re-check for updates. [Image: source: /Users/ericpardee/Downloads/IMG_5753.PNG]
Found the cause. The Fire HD 10's hardware H.264 decoder is being overwhelmed. Look at these errors repeating: The cameras are using **WebRTC streams**, and the MT8183 SoC in your Fire HD 10 can only handle ~1-2 simultaneous 1080p H.264 decode streams. Your dashboard is showing 5+ cameras at once, which saturates the decoder. When frames fail to decode, WebRTC renders the default fallback color: **green**. Your phone handles it fine because it has a beefier decoder. ## Fixes, in order of effectiveness **1. Switch those camera cards to `picture-entity` (easiest)** Change the Nest/Wyze cards from streaming/WebRTC cards to `picture-entity` with a snapshot refresh (e.g., every 5-10s). No decoder load, no green screens. You'll lose live video but get auto-refreshing stills. **2. Reduce simultaneous streams** Only put 1-2 WebRTC streams on the main dashboard. Move the rest to sub-dashboards where you click through one at a time. **3. Try disabling hardware acceleration in Fully Kiosk** In Fully Kiosk: **Settings 
 Web Content Settings 
 Hardware Acceleration 
 OFF**. The software decoder is slower but more reliable, and without HW decoding the green-fallback issue usually disappears. **4. Lower the stream resolution for the tablet** If you're using go2rtc, add a lower-res stream profile for the kiosk view (e.g., 480p). Less work for the decoder. plugged in** because it's only receiving 500ma but consuming ~700ma with the screen on and fully kiosk running. the charger is being detected as a basic usb source instead of a wall charger. when the battery eventually hits 0%, it powers off completely. the battery stats confirm this pattern   the charge level bounces around but trends downward over days until shutdown. ## why it started 4 months ago after years of 24 7 connection, the **cable, charger, or usb port** has degraded enough that proper power negotiation fails. the charger's data pins (d+ d ) are how the tablet identifies it as a wall charger vs basic usb. when those degrade, it falls back to 500ma usb spec. ## fixes (in order of priority) **1. replace the cable first** (cheapest, most likely culprit). get a quality usb c cable that supports at least 2a. **2. replace the charger** if a new cable doesn't fix it. any 5v 2a+ adapter will work, but the amazon 9w original equivalent is ideal. **3. clean the usb c port** on cond ret: 0 0 0 0 vbat:3916 here's the full picture now: ## diagnosis: amazon's software is shutting down your tablet the evidence is clear:   **shutdown reason**: `software shutdown` (confirmed in kernel logs, dropbox, and vitals)   **boot reason**: `reboot,userrequested` (system software initiated)   **last session**: ran for ~20.4 hours before shutdown   **the ota service** (`com.amazon.device.software.ota`) is actively running on every boot   **a forced ota package** (`com.amazon.kindle.otter.oobe.forced.ota`) exists as a privileged system app listening for `start forced ota` intents   your `ota disable automatic update=1` setting is being overridden or bypassed amazon is almost certainly triggering periodic shutdowns as part of a failed ota update cycle. it tries to install an update, shuts the device down to apply it, and since updates are blocked unavailable, the device just stays off. ## fix: disable the ota packages via adb want me to run these commands to disable the offending packages? ``` adb shell pm disable user   user 0 com.amazon.device.software.ota adb shell pm disable user   user 0 com.amazon.device.software.ota.override adb shell pm disable user   user 0 com.amazon.kindle.otter.oobe.forced.ota adb shell pm disable user   user 0 com.amazon.settings.systemupdates ``` this will prevent the ota services from running without needing root, and is reversible if you ever want to re enable them. it won't affect anything else on the device since you're using it as a dedicated kiosk. yes exit code 255 security exception: cannot service has **`android.permission.reboot`** and **`android.permission.recovery`** as privileged permissions. these cannot be revoked without root. amazon's ota service can shut down your device at will, and there's no adb level way to stop it on a non rooted fire tablet. ## here's the situation and your real options **why it can't be fixed without root:** the ota packages are "protected" at the framework level. can't disable them, can't uninstall them, can't revoke their reboot permission. amazon designed it this way. ### option 1: block ota at the network level (easiest, no root needed) since you already run home assistant, you likely have access to your router or run something like adguard pi hole. block these domains:   `softwareupdates.amazon.com`   `device software updates us.amazon.com`   `firs ta g7g.amazon.com`   `amzn ota eu.amazon.com`   `amzn ota na.amazon.com` if the ota service can't reach amazon's servers, it shouldn't trigger a shutdown to apply a non existent update. ### option 2: root the device (permanent fix) your tablet has a mediatek mt8183 soc (confirmed from kernel logs). the fire hd 10 11th gen ("trona") has known root methods via `mtk su` or the `amonet` bootloader exploit. once rooted, we can fully remove the ota packages from the system partition. ### option 3: smart plug auto recovery (workaround) use a smart plug controlled by home assistant. monitor the tablet (via ping or fully kiosk's rest api), and if it goes offline, power cycle the plug. fire tablets auto boot when power is reconnected. which approach do you want to go decodable frame in 200 ms, requesting keyframe. found the cause. the fire hd 10's hardware h.264 decoder is being overwhelmed. look at these errors repeating: ``` too many frames backed up in the decoder, dropping frame failed to decode frame, error code:  1 no decodable frame in 200 ms, requesting keyframe ``` the cameras are using **webrtc streams**, and the mt8183 soc in your fire hd 10 can only handle ~1 2 simultaneous 1080p h.264 decode streams. your dashboard is showing 5+ cameras at once, which saturates the decoder. when frames fail to decode, webrtc renders the default fallback color: **green**. your phone handles it fine because it has a beefier decoder. ## fixes, in order of effectiveness **1. switch those camera cards to `picture entity` (easiest)** change the nest wyze cards from streaming webrtc cards to `picture entity` with a snapshot refresh (e.g., every 5 10s). no decoder load, no green screens. you'll lose live video but get auto refreshing stills. **2. reduce simultaneous streams** only put 1 2 webrtc streams on the main dashboard. move the rest to sub dashboards where you click through one at a time. **3. try disabling hardware acceleration in fully kiosk** in fully kiosk: **settings 
 web content settings 
 hardware acceleration 
 off**. the software decoder is slower but more reliable, and without hw decoding the green fallback issue usually disappears. **4. lower the stream resolution for the tablet** if you're using go2rtc, add a lower res stream profile for the kiosk view (e.g., 480p). less work for the decoder. want me to pull your current fully kiosk config via adb to check if hardware acceleration is currently on?
